I hold that a mode which permanently deletes in-call audio on a weak end signal is not a privacy feature, and that short retention plus an unsynced library is the honest version; it is the owner's call either way.
6. Scope of I01. Others may prefer I15's whole-app airgap or I07's broker. I keep the Python child only: by inference from the audit digest, FluidAudio 0.14.8 under a network deny turns a model load error into a deleted cache with no refetch, and the profile filters surprised me twice in a few minutes on this host.
7. I33. Judges looking for the interesting answer may rank it first. I back the internal verify-the-fix step now and publication only after a privacy read of the history.
8. I36 at fit 5 with feasibility 3. A stricter feasibility judge would drop it until G003 is fixed; I keep it because it is the owner's stated pain and a three-day stdlib script decides whether the vectors cluster.

### owner-advocate

Top picks: I35 (A yardstick that needs no labelling: channel energy, correction overlays and glossary firings as a standing speech scorecard), I42 (Flip the default on the secondary surfaces: deleted on a date unless a local witness shows use), I41 (Regulated mode means zero egress, not zero recovery: delete the real-time gate and the destructive end cut), I46 (Stop producing claims faster than one person can check them: audit moratorium, a witness rule, and deletion of unfenced prose), I32 (Upstream first: give FluidAudio the background-agent, long-form, bilingual regression case instead of extracting anything), I36 (Voice census: name a voice once and the archive resolves), I16 (The archive as a build graph: every artifact re-derivable from retained audio, so the whole library improves when a model does), I01 (Kernel-enforced zero egress with a per-meeting egress receipt).

Direction in one sentence: stop adding, make the existing claims true by deletion wherever deletion is possible, and aim everything that does get built at the one property the funded field has given up, an archive that keeps its audio, gets more accurate with age and knows who spoke.

Why this and not a rewrite or a new surface. The market file says the base loop is a commodity and that more parity features will not make the tool distinctive (https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/items/50454625), that retained audio is the one asset the best-funded botless vendor chose not to keep (https://www.granola.ai/pricing, https://platformer.news/granola-chris-pedregal-interview), and that accurate names, numbers and speaker identity are what users rank first (https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map). The audit says breadth outruns proven reliability, that data handling is the weakest dimension, and that 18 fixes recorded as done do not hold. Both point the same way: a smaller product whose few claims (kept audio, named voices, nothing leaves) are measurably true.

Month 1, subtract and fix. Adopt I46 on day one: no new audit and no new task until critical and high are zero, and a fix closes only on a witness. Work the audit's fix list, choosing deletion over repair: I41 for G001 and F004 (needs the owner's decision on ADR 0016), I42's immediate cuts (series continuity, which removes F014, plus Facts, the prep card, App Intents and the LAN sink) and its 14-day witness for the remaining surfaces, I43 for F017 and F088, and then the fixes that cannot be deleted (G003, F001 with F020, F012, F005, F046). Two one-day counts ride along and end in delete commits: I47, and the deletion half of I44. In the same weeks run three owner-run, read-only measurements that each cost minutes of owner time: I32 first (Neural Engine in the menu-bar agent on 27.2), I35 steps 0 to 3, and I36 parts A and B.

Month 2, measure and decide. Qualify FluidAudio 0.18.3 on the yardstick diff, which is the first consumer of I16. Build time-keyed overlay re-anchoring, which is both the F062 fix and the precondition for any reprint. Run two more bounded measurements: the 3 to 5 day LOCAL12 bench (stage zero of I17) so the ARCH11 boundary ADR is written with a number in it, and I01's 5-day sandbox probe with I31's F014 replay leg added. Build the canary fence and the MCP usage count from I08 as a standing CI fence. If capacity remains, take I29's schemas and counts-only validator as ARCH10's typed owner.

Month 3, build one thing. If I36's separability bar passes, build the name-once queue (three clips, one name field, one undo for the whole fan-out). If the yardstick shows a win, stamp derivations and run one speech-only reprint in I38's shape: previous print kept, published notes untouched. Nothing else is built. I45 may run all quarter as a reversible setting on one or two workflows, because it costs no code.

What I would refuse this quarter. Rust anywhere in the product (I07, I14, I20), with I34 allowed only as an out-of-tree hobby after I29; the market file's own reading is that Rust buys cross-platform reach and costs the Apple accelerators (https://moklabs-research.pages.dev/reports/technology/2026-03-20-parakeet-rs-tauri-integration-patterns). Starting the all-Swift port before the bench has run and before the six Python highs are fixed. A second process for capture (I18) beyond its TCC probe. The always-armed microphone (I27). Encryption of the withheld tier (I09). Any MCP write tool (I11). Ingest from phones or other recorders (I19, I25) and wider call sensing (I24). Legal-persona features: I02's gating ledger, I03's hold and certificates, I04's clocks, I05's evidence bundles. Person pages as the Library's home (I39) and briefs (I12) until the census reports. Publishing anything (I28, I31, I33) while open data-loss and privacy defects are on the list. And turning this list of 47 ideas into 47 backlog tasks.

**Where this judge expects to be outvoted.** 1. All-Swift (I17). I expect the technical judges to rank the staged port as the headline radical move, and the assessor already recommends it conditionally. I agree it is the only rewrite that removes a measured cost, and I want the bench run this quarter. I hold that the port itself must not start yet: the record costs it at about 21k lines and 3 to 6 months, it lands in the tree at 48% coverage, and the owner's review time is already overdrawn by 18 fixes that do not hold. A differential oracle on golden fixtures helps only for the roughly 10 of 30 contracts that are pinned.

2. The egress cluster (I01, I07, I15, I31). Market-minded judges will put kernel-enforced zero egress with per-meeting receipts first, since the market file finds no rival that prints proof per meeting. I rank only I01's child-only wrap, and eighth, because the leaks the audit actually proved (F014, F005, F046, F018) are policy-layer and sit upstream of any network deny, the mechanism is deprecated, and a receipt written by the code it attests invites overclaiming in a codebase whose documented weakness is overclaiming. The canary fence comes first.

3. Consent ledger and custody chain (I02, I05), and the retention and biometric registers (I03, I04). These will score high on differentiation and they are real open ground. I score them low on fit because they serve a lawyer, clinician or works council the owner is not, each adds a gate or a file kind every later feature must honour, and I04 works against the naming the owner wants more of.

4. Agent memory and person pages (I08's tiers, I10, I11, I12, I39). Others will see the context layer as where the field is going. I hold that MCP usage on this Mac is unmeasured, naming covers a third of voices, and the commitments loop was already rejected by real use; count first, then build at most I10.

5. Subtraction at the top of the list. A panel asked for radical ideas may find three deletions, a moratorium and a yardstick timid. My view is that for one reviewer the radical act is to shrink, and that none of the larger bets can be reviewed until the surface is smaller. The interesting product is the one whose few claims are measurably true.

6. I45 and I33. I expect some judges to place archive-first or publish-the-experiment in their top three as the truly contrarian answers. I rate both as worth a cheap trial and neither as this quarter's direction: I45 reverses the stated core job on the strength of a habit that does not exist yet, and I33 serves readers other than the owner.

7. I41. Privacy-first judges may refuse to keep muted audio at rest for NDA meetings. I hold that a permanent hole in the most sensitive recordings is the worse failure, provided the reaper fix and the move off iCloud come first and the owner signs the ADR change.

Process note: the advisor tool was rate-limited during this run, so these scores had no second review.

## Radical options and their verdicts

From the technical assessor, who read the source and the project's earlier spikes and judged each option against the pain the audit actually found.

Short version: roughly nine tenths of what the audit found would survive any change of language or process layout, and a rewrite would re-roll that part in fresh AI-written code under the same single reviewer.

Three buckets, by cause.

(A) Caused by the Swift/Python split, so a one-language or one-owner layout would have prevented them: F022 and F023 (Regenerate and Retry send Apple Intelligence meetings to a Python summarizer that refuses them), F108 and F125 (the Swift Apple summarizer is a second, narrower summarize stage), F106 (Swift writes summary.json shapes the Python reader rejects), F109 (failure reason on stdout while the daemon reads stderr), F123 (preview gated on the global backend while Python resolves the workflow backend), F126 (title precedence opposite in the two trees), G033 (setup checklist and doctor disagree), G034 (two sink rules for regulated mode), F129 (about 10 of 30 cross-tree contracts fixture-pinned), F021 (a September binary driving HEAD Python), F055 and F143 (pipe and stderr-tail plumbing), plus the already-filed LOCAL21 (two owners of mlx_lm.server). That is about fourteen findings, all medium, around 7 percent of the 194. I read F021, F022, F023, F107, F108, F125, F129 and G034 in full and classified the others from their titles and the contracts and x-architecture slice notes.

(B) Architectural, but inside the Swift tree, so only a module redesign helps and a language change does not: F049 (the 1,980-line session controller with 29 mutable flags), which is where three of the sixteen highs sit (F004, F005, F046) along with F045; F110 and F151 (no test starts and stops a real recording because the recorder has no headless seam; real daemon coverage is 51 percent, the recorder 17 percent, Coordinator 0.3 percent); and the 71k-line single executable target with no access control, which is what lets a view rewrite a sidecar (F007, F061, F063 and F066 share the read-as-absent-then-rewrite shape).

(C) Not architectural: the critical G001 and the highs are logic, lifecycle and data-model errors inside one tree. By location 10 are Swift, 6 are Python (F014, F016, F017, F018, F019, F088) and 1 is uninstall.sh (F020). F019 and G004 have a cross-tree flavour (overlays and name keys that one tree owns and the other ignores), but the fix is one owner for the rule, not one language. Of the 18 fixes that do not hold, only F107 and F109 touch the seam. Docs that overstate code (F041, G032) and tests that pin the defect (G001, G006, G024, G025) are verification failures.

Two facts cut against any rewrite. The Python tree is the better instrumented half (90 percent line coverage, 1,559 tests, one egress chokepoint) and the Swift app target the weaker one (47.9 percent), so a port moves code toward the thinner net unless it lands in a new island target. And the audit's sharpest pattern, claims outrunning code, is a shortage of owner verification, which a large rewrite consumes rather than supplies.

| Option | Verdict | Effort |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Rust core for the whole engine | Do not | About 78k lines of Swift and 23k of Python to re-derive, plus the intent of 86k lines of tests. Agents can write that in weeks; the binding cost is field verification by one person. The end-of-call fixes END9 to END17 are still awaiting fie [...] |
| 2. Rust for one accelerator-free component (index and search, MCP server, or transcript store) | Experiment only | Out-of-tree reader: 2 to 4 agent days and one owner evening to read the gap list. In-app integration: about 2 weeks plus permanent upkeep of a third language by one reviewer; not recommended. |
| 3. All-Swift, dropping the Python sidecar, staged | Stage it | After the deletions in option 4, about 18k lines of Python and the intent of 1,559 tests. The go-live assessment costed the full port at about 21k lines and 3 to 6 months solo, and the cloud-plus-publish half at about 10k lines and 6 to 10 [...] |
| 4. Keep two trees, shrink the seam (fewer subcommands, one summarizer, generated contracts) | Do | Seven to nine single-commit tasks in the project's existing task shape. Agent time 1 to 2 days each; owner review 30 to 60 minutes each plus normal dogfooding. Three to five weeks calendar when interleaved with the audit's data-handling fix [...] |
| 5. Cross-platform engine (Windows or Linux) | Do not | Strictly larger than option 1, which it contains, plus a second capture and sensing implementation and a UI toolkit decision PRODUCT.md currently forbids (no web-rendered chrome). I would not estimate it under a year for one reviewer. |
| 6. iOS or watch companion | Do not | Four to eight weeks for a record-and-hand-off companion with sync, then permanent upkeep of a second app and a second OS release cycle. |
| 7. Headless daemon with thin clients | Stage it | Stage 1: about 280 files to assign and one god object to untangle; 2 to 3 weeks of small landings. This is the rare large change one reviewer can trust without reading every line, because access-control violations fail the build. Stage 2: a [...] |

### 1. Rust core for the whole engine: do not

Replace the engine in both trees with a Rust core under a thin Swift shell. On this product the engine is mostly Apple frameworks: ScreenCaptureKit and the mic tap for capture, CoreAudio HAL and Accessibility for call sensing, FluidAudio CoreML models on the Neural Engine for ASR and diarization, FoundationModels for the system model. Rust reaches each of those only through a Swift or Objective-C bridge, so the result is three layers (Rust core, Swift bridge, SwiftUI) and a new FFI seam of exactly the kind the audit found expensive. Every notable Rust recorder in the market file chose Rust for cross-platform reach, which this project lists as a non-goal. Not previously decided by the project; the nearest record is ADR 0006, which rejected a second language for a helper on one-language grounds.

**Fixes.** None directly. It would dissolve the Swift/Python seam findings (F021, F022, F023, F106, F108, F109, F123, F125, F126, F129, G033, G034) only by replacing that seam with a Rust/Swift one.

**Does not fix.** G001 and every high finding (they are logic and would be re-derived from scratch); the 18 fixes that do not hold; F110 and F151; F049 would be rebuilt rather than removed. It discards MeetingPipeCore (7,250 lines, 90 percent covered, strict concurrency), one of the two islands the audit calls sound, and the 3,531 Swift tests.

**Effort.** About 78k lines of Swift and 23k of Python to re-derive, plus the intent of 86k lines of tests. Agents can write that in weeks; the binding cost is field verification by one person. The end-of-call fixes END9 to END17 are still awaiting field confirmation months after they shipped on the existing code, and a rewrite restarts that clock for capture, detection, mute handling and post-processing at once. Realistic calendar to regain today's reliability: 9 to 12 months, with no differential oracle for the capture and sensing half.

**Risks.** Losing Neural Engine Parakeet: the market file's source reports the ONNX Runtime CoreML provider crashing on Parakeet inference and calls CPU the safe default, and mlx-rs describes itself as in active development with segfault warnings. Keeping FluidAudio means keeping Swift anyway. Capture is where teams move toward Swift, not away (Circleback rebuilt its recording engine in Swift). The whole regression net is gone during the period when the audit says trust is already the problem.

**First step.** None recommended. If the owner wants one number before closing it: a one-day throwaway outside the repo, parakeet-rs on a public test clip on this Mac, wall time and text compared with FluidAudio on the same clip. That measures the accelerator gap directly.

**Evidence relied on.** Market file, tech-frontier segment: https://moklabs-research.pages.dev/reports/technology/2026-03-20-parakeet-rs-tauri-integration-patterns , https://github.com/oxiglade/mlx-rs , https://lib.rs/crates/parakeet-rs , https://circleback.ai/blog/how-we-rebuilt-our-electron-recording-engine-in-swift . Repo: the repo root daemon/Package.swift (FluidAudio and TOMLKit are the only Swift dependencies), wc on daemon/Sources and pipeline/src, audit scorecard and build facts in the repo root docs/audits/2026-11-12-independent-audit.md . I did not reproduce the Rust accelerator claims.

### 2. Rust for one accelerator-free component (index and search, MCP server, or transcript store): experiment only

As a product move this fails the audit's own test: the main architectural cost is that two languages each hold a copy of some rule, and this adds a third. Candidate by candidate. Search is a 500-line Swift SQLite FTS5 cache in a dimension the audit rated 4.5 with nothing above medium, and its semantic half (embeddings) runs on MLX, so it is not accelerator-free. The MCP server is about 930 lines of Python including the visibility gate, and a Rust version would be a third implementation of a privacy rule in a codebase whose privacy findings (F014, F123, G034 and the open AUTO6) are all one rule applied at one site and missed at another. A transcript store either contradicts ADR 0003 (files are the source of truth) or duplicates the MeetingFiles owner type ARCH10 asks for. What changed since ADR 0006 rejected a Go helper on one-language grounds: nothing in its favour; the audit strengthened the one-language argument. The only shape worth running is an experiment that never ships inside the app: an out-of-tree, read-only Rust reader of the library written from CONVENTIONS.md alone, as a conformance test of the open on-disk format the market file names as a structural advantage.

**Fixes.** None. As a side effect the conformance experiment would surface documentation gaps of the F129, F041 and G032 kind (docs claiming more than the code delivers) in the sidecar and summary schemas.

**Does not fix.** Every finding. If integrated into the app it makes the seam metrics worse: a third toolchain, a cargo leg in install.sh, rebuild.sh and ci-local.sh (SwiftPM cannot build Rust), and either an FFI boundary or one more subprocess contract.

**Effort.** Out-of-tree reader: 2 to 4 agent days and one owner evening to read the gap list. In-app integration: about 2 weeks plus permanent upkeep of a third language by one reviewer; not recommended.

**Risks.** A third copy of the visibility gate if the MCP server is the target. Scope creep from experiment to dependency. The experiment must run on a synthetic fixture library, never the live one.

**First step.** Write the pass condition first: the reader lists meetings, resolves speakers through the overlays and prints a summary with its evidence anchors for a synthetic fixture library, using only CONVENTIONS.md and ARCHITECTURE.md as its specification. Every question the agent can only answer by opening Swift or Python source is logged as a format-documentation defect.

**Evidence relied on.** the repo root daemon/Sources/MeetingPipe/SearchIndex.swift (308 lines) and SearchIndexer.swift (194); the repo root pipeline/src/mp/mcp_serve.py (453), mcp_tools.py (307), visibility.py (167), embed_index.py (MLX embedder); ADR 0003 and ADR 0006 as recorded in the ideas digest; audit scorecard (performance 4.5). Market file on durability as an advantage: https://blog.superhuman.com/superhuman-acquires-fathom/ and https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map .

### 3. All-Swift, dropping the Python sidecar, staged: stage it

The only rewrite on the list that removes a cost class the audit measured. The shape I would recommend is a second Swift target in the same package, built as a separate worker executable, not Python logic folded into the menu-bar process: one language, one build artifact, contract types shared by import, while LLM and network work stay out of the process that holds the microphone and screen-capture grants and out of the blast radius of a model that runs out of memory (the AI2 spike recorded a 14B model crashing this Mac). It should not start until one measurement is in. What changed since the July NO-GO: its premise was that the candidate did not exist and building it was the risk; the market file reports mlx-swift-lm 3.32.3 with guided generation, Qwen support and a Foundation Models conformer, and my reading of the repo shows the Python baseline has no constrained decoding either (details in the recommended path). Port order if the measurement passes: the local generation server first at the existing OpenAI-compatible HTTP seam (highest risk first, and it is the spike artifact promoted), then summarize and providers behind one egress chokepoint, then orchestration, publishers, library verbs, embeddings and Ask, and MCP. Each leg deletes its Python twin in the same landing and is checked differentially against Python's output on the existing golden fixtures before the deletion, which gives one reviewer an executable oracle instead of a reading task.

**Fixes.** F022, F023, F108, F125 (one summarizer, Apple Intelligence reduced to a provider); F106 (one typed writer for summary.json); F123, F126, G033, G034 (one copy of each rule); F129 (no cross-language fixtures to keep in step); F021 (one artifact, so binary and pipeline cannot skew); LOCAL21 (no mlx_lm.server to co-own); the open SEC31 (13 advisories in a 102-package lock); the 400 MB embedded runtime DIST1 plans; the uv and Homebrew Python prerequisites. F055, F109 and F143 only if the worker returns a typed result envelope, which option 4 delivers sooner.

**Does not fix.** The critical G001 and ten highs are already Swift (F004, F005, F009, F011, F012, F046, G003, G004, F001) or shell (F020). The six Python highs (F014, F016, F017, F018, F019, F088) are logic a faithful port would carry over unless they are fixed and pinned first. F049, F110, F151 and the 18 fixes that do not hold are untouched.

**Effort.** After the deletions in option 4, about 18k lines of Python and the intent of 1,559 tests. The go-live assessment costed the full port at about 21k lines and 3 to 6 months solo, and the cloud-plus-publish half at about 10k lines and 6 to 10 weeks. With agents writing, typing is days per leg and the unit of cost is an owner verification week per leg. Seven legs: 3 to 5 months calendar. The gate before any of it: 2 to 3 agent days to assemble the Swift candidate, plus one owner evening to hand-grade summaries against the four bars LOCAL12 pre-registered. Note that the baseline half of that bench has been owner-owed since July.

**Risks.** (1) Moving code from the 90 percent covered tree into the 48 percent covered target; acceptable only as a new island target with MeetingPipeCore's rules and tests ported one for one. (2) Egress: Python has one httpx transport to guard. Swift needs one URLSession factory plus a source fence, and vendor adapters behind Apple's LanguageModel protocol own their own transport, the same risk class as the httpx2 note in pyproject.toml; PrivateCloudComputeLanguageModel must be fenced out of regulated and NDA runs. (3) mlx-swift-lm churn: a breaking 3.x in April and an instability warning in June per the market file; pin exactly, as FluidAudio is pinned. (4) Build path: the market file says the Metal shader library needs an Xcode build, and install.sh, rebuild.sh and ci-local.sh all call plain swift build today. (5) The LanguageModel protocol is OS 27 only while Package.swift floors at macOS 14. (6) Unknowns that could each sink a leg: Qwen tokenizer and chat-template parity, a Swift equivalent of multilingual-e5-small for Ask, a Swift MCP SDK current with the 2026-07-28 specification, and replacing pydantic's scrub and limits with hand-written validation. (7) A new dependency needs owner approval under the project rules.

**First step.** Build the candidate LOCAL12 said did not exist, outside the app target: a throwaway Xcode-built Swift CLI that loads the configured Qwen2.5-7B-4bit through mlx-swift-lm, constrains output to the MeetingSummary schema with guided generation, and serves the same OpenAI-compatible endpoint. The owner then runs both halves of docs/spikes/local12_bench.py (baseline and candidate) on the same three transcripts, one long enough to hit map-reduce, and grades against the four pre-registered bars. Record build friction (Xcode requirement, dependency count) in the same note.

**Evidence relied on.** the repo root docs/spikes/dist2-swift-port-of-the-pipeline.md ; the repo root docs/spikes/local12-mlx-swift-trigger-evaluation.md ; the repo root docs/decisions/0007-python-sidecar.md ; the repo root docs/backlog/tasks/I7.md and ARCH11.md ; the repo root docs/audits/2026-10-21-go-live-assessment.md (lines 74 to 75) ; the repo root pipeline/src/mp/summarize_local.py (lines 17 to 29 and 1211 to 1222) against the repo root pipeline/.venv/lib/python3.11/site-packages/mlx_lm/server.py (no response_format handling) ; pipeline/pyproject.toml (httpx cap comment) ; daemon/Package.swift (macOS 14 floor). Market file: https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx-swift-lm/releases , https://developer.apple.com/documentation/updates/foundationmodels.md , https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2026/343/ , https://infoq.com/news/2026/06/apple-core-ai-wwdc , https://instagit.com/Zackriya-Solutions/meetily/meetily-tauri-app-vs-fastapi-backend/ .

### 4. Keep two trees, shrink the seam (fewer subcommands, one summarizer, generated contracts): do

The cheapest route to retiring the seam findings, and useful whichever way option 3 goes, provided the legs are sorted by whether they depend on that direction. Direction-neutral, do now: (a) delete unwired and dev-only Python, about 2.9k of 22.8k lines (summarize_staged.py 994, analyze_detection.py 756, train_adapter.py 526, dogfood.py 469, logs_cmd.py 131), plus whatever FEAT18 cuts; every deleted line is a line never ported or mirrored; (b) LOCAL21, one owner for the local model server, removing about 850 lines of lsof and ps reconciliation across local_server.py, LocalServerReaper.swift and LocalModelPreloader.swift; (c) DIST6 plus a spawn-time handshake so the daemon and the pipeline it launches must carry the same commit; (d) ARCH10's single writer for per-meeting files and a JSON result envelope from mp in place of exit-code and stderr inference; (e) contract fixtures generated from the pydantic models for every cross-tree surface rather than 10 of about 30, and ARCH14's typed backend; (f) one rule each for regulated-mode sinks and title precedence, with Swift reading the resolved policy from the sidecar instead of re-deriving it; (g) collapsing the 16 daemon-invoked verbs behind that envelope. Direction-dependent, hold: ARCH11's provider leg as written moves Apple Intelligence summarization under Python, which is throwaway if option 3 proceeds. The neutral fix for F022 and F023 in the meantime is the audit's own direction: resolve the effective backend in Swift before a nil-backend regenerate.

**Fixes.** F021 (handshake), F022 and F023 (routing), F106 and F109 (single writer, result envelope), F123 (typed backend), F126 and G034 (one rule), G033 (one reachability model), F129 (generated fixtures), F083 (if the trainer is deleted), LOCAL21. F108 and F125 only once the summarizer question is decided one way or the other. Partial: F107, by giving overlay application one owner that publishers must call.

**Does not fix.** Two languages for one reviewer; the Python runtime, its 102-package lock and 13 advisories; install weight. None of the critical or high findings.

**Effort.** Seven to nine single-commit tasks in the project's existing task shape. Agent time 1 to 2 days each; owner review 30 to 60 minutes each plus normal dogfooding. Three to five weeks calendar when interleaved with the audit's data-handling fixes, which should keep priority. The deletions are hours.

**Risks.** Low. Doing ARCH11's Python-owns leg and then porting wastes it. The envelope touches every spawn site in PipelineLauncher.swift (1,690 lines, 63 percent covered, and its one subprocess test does not run in CI), so that leg should put the subprocess test into CI first. Contract generation should stay a script that emits fixtures, not a new code-generator dependency. The LoRA trainer is a separate owner call: LOCAL17 keeps a retry trigger for it, while F083 says it is wrong as written.

**First step.** Land PIPE20's delete-or-verdict call on the unwired staged summarizer and the dev tooling, then DIST6's commit stamp with a spawn-time comparison. Both are small, reversible in git, and remove surface before anything else is touched.

**Evidence relied on.** the repo root docs/backlog/tasks/ARCH11.md , ARCH10.md , ARCH14.md , LOCAL21.md , DIST6.md , PIPE20.md ; wc over the repo root pipeline/src/mp ; grep of mp verbs at Swift spawn sites (16 distinct, matching the audit) ; audit findings F021, F022, F023, F108, F125, F129, G034 read in full in the repo root docs/audits/2026-11-12-independent-audit.md . No market claim relied on.

### 5. Cross-platform engine (Windows or Linux): do not

The README lists cross-platform as a non-goal by design. What changed since: competitors went there (the market file has Talat shipping a Windows build, Anarlog adding Windows and Linux betas, Meetily on Mac and Windows), and the same file reads that as reach, not quality. Nothing changed on the owner's side: one user, one Mac, Teams-native calls. The part of this product that is hardest won and still unproven, call lifecycle fusion from Accessibility, per-process CoreAudio activity, ScreenCaptureKit windows and the Teams log, has no portable core; each OS needs its own sensing and capture layer, and there is no Neural Engine off Apple hardware.

**Fixes.** None.

**Does not fix.** Everything in the audit. It multiplies the weakest-tested area (recorder at 17 percent, capture lifecycle pinned by source-text fences, F110) by the number of platforms.

**Effort.** Strictly larger than option 1, which it contains, plus a second capture and sensing implementation and a UI toolkit decision PRODUCT.md currently forbids (no web-rendered chrome). I would not estimate it under a year for one reviewer.

**Risks.** Turns one unfinished product into two. It only pays off with distribution to other people, which sits on the not-for-sale list (signing, installers), so mark it as crossing that line. End-of-call detection is partial on one platform and calibrated to English-UI Teams.

**First step.** None. If the underlying wish is that the archive outlives the Mac app, the format-conformance reader in option 2 delivers the portable part, the data, without porting the engine.

**Evidence relied on.** Ideas digest (README non-goals; END9 to END17 partial; DET15). Market file: https://hackernoon.com/six-months-with-tauri-the-benefits-and-the-bill , https://instagit.com/Zackriya-Solutions/meetily/meetily-tauri-app-vs-fastapi-backend/ , https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/24/talats-ai-meeting-notes-stay-on-your-machine-not-in-the-cloud/ . Audit coverage figures for MeetingRecorder.swift and SystemAudioCapture.swift.

### 6. iOS or watch companion: do not

The README lists a mobile companion as a non-goal. What changed since: Granola shipped Apple Watch and Android apps, Talat shipped iPhone and Watch apps, and Zoom and Google added in-person notes, so in-person capture is where funded players are expanding; the market file's own advice for a solo local tool is not to chase it. On the owner's side the record shows Teams-native meetings and no measured in-person need. Technically it is a second product: an Xcode project and provisioning (the repo builds with plain swift build), a transfer channel to the Mac when two-Mac sync is itself deferred P3, and none of MeetingPipeCore applies on iOS. Same-room single-microphone audio is the weakest diarization case per the market file, and phone-class summarizers lose the thread on long meetings.

**Fixes.** None.

**Does not fix.** Everything in the audit. It adds a third surface while 41 shipped tasks still await field confirmation.

**Effort.** Four to eight weeks for a record-and-hand-off companion with sync, then permanent upkeep of a second app and a second OS release cycle.

**Risks.** Crosses the not-for-sale list: a build that stays installed on a phone needs a paid developer identity and provisioning, and CloudKit sync needs entitlements, which is the signing line. Audio in transit through a cloud relay cuts against the privacy identity (the library already reaches iCloud unintentionally, the SEC12 warning). Voiceprints leaving the Mac are the sharpest legal edge in the market file.

**First step.** Measure demand before code: a two-week tally of in-person conversations the owner wished had been captured. If it is not zero, the cheapest probe needs no iOS code and no signing: an import-an-audio-file path on the Mac fed by the phone's built-in recorder, which reuses the whole pipeline and shows whether single-microphone diarization is usable at all.

**Evidence relied on.** Ideas digest (README non-goals; two-Mac hub deferred P3; calendar and browser meetings unused by this owner). Market file: https://granola.ai/updates , https://hedy.ai/post/local-ai-engineering-deep-dive-hedy-3-2/ , https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map . Repo: grep of the repo root daemon/Sources found no audio-file import path; scripts/install.sh and rebuild.sh build with swift build.

### 7. Headless daemon with thin clients: stage it

Take the module boundary now and the process boundary only behind two measurements. The audit's Swift-internal pain comes from one 71k-line executable target with no access control and a recorder with no headless seam; a SwiftPM target split fixes both without a second process. Stage 1: split the executable into library targets, an engine (session, recorder, post-processing, pipeline launch; no SwiftUI), a library-files target (ARCH10's MeetingFiles and single writers), and the UI on top, so the compiler enforces what text-grep fences police today, and turn the in-flight-start Bool and the 29 flags into the explicit state machine ARCH7 describes. Stage 2: a headless entry point over the engine so one integration test can start, write and stop a real recording from a synthetic source. Stage 3, the actual launchd agent with XPC clients, stays closed until (a) a UI-less process is shown to get the Neural Engine on macOS 27.2 and (b) the binaries have a stable signing identity. A Swift-to-Swift XPC contract is far cheaper than the Python seam because both sides import the same Codable types, but it is still a new seam. Not previously decided by the project; ARCH7 is the nearest filed task.

**Fixes.** Stages 1 and 2: F110 (a real recording under test), F049 with ARCH7 (and through it the conditions behind F004, F005, F045 and F046), the access-control gap the x-architecture slice names, F007, F061, F063 and F066 via single writers, and it gives F151's real coverage figure somewhere to rise. Stage 3 fixes no audit finding; it answers a market wish (Hacker News users asking for a headless daemon with a CLI) and would let a UI fault leave a recording running.

**Does not fix.** G001 (policy logic); F012 unless ConfigStore gets reload-before-write semantics; every Python finding; the UI state findings F009, F011, F069 and F071.

**Effort.** Stage 1: about 280 files to assign and one god object to untangle; 2 to 3 weeks of small landings. This is the rare large change one reviewer can trust without reading every line, because access-control violations fail the build. Stage 2: about a week. Stage 3: 4 to 6 weeks plus signing, not recommended now.

**Risks.** Stage 1 collides with concurrent fix work in the same files, so sequence it by directory after the top data-handling fixes. About 90 source-text fence tests break on file moves; several are vacuous per the audit and should be replaced by the stage 2 test rather than repaired. Stage 3: microphone, screen and Accessibility grants attach to a binary identity, so a second binary under ad-hoc signing doubles the permission churn, and the dependable fix is Developer ID, which crosses the not-for-sale list. The audit measured Neural Engine dispatch only from a foreground CLI; FluidAudio issue 738 quotes Apple restricting background access on OS 27, and a launchd agent with no UI is further into that risk than today's menu-bar agent.

**First step.** One owner-run measurement that also answers the audit's open question about the app as it runs today: log the MLComputePlan dispatch for the Parakeet encoder from the real menu-bar agent and from a UI-less launchd-started process on macOS 27.2, beside the foreground numbers the audit already has (encoder 1,381 ops on the Neural Engine). Then start stage 1 with the smallest target, the per-meeting file contract.

**Evidence relied on.** the repo root docs/backlog/tasks/ARCH7.md and ARCH10.md ; audit slice notes for spine, tests-swift, x-architecture and the fluidaudio_on_host build fact in the repo root docs/audits/2026-11-12-independent-audit.md ; the repo root daemon/Package.swift (one executable target plus Core and ObjC) ; the repo root docs/backlog/tasks/ASR6.md (issue 738 tracked only in a manifest comment). Market file: https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/items/50454625 for the headless-daemon wish; https://github.com/FluidInference/FluidAudio/releases for the macOS 27 fixes.

### Recommended path

Do option 4's direction-neutral legs now, run two measurements in parallel, and let the first measurement decide between option 3 and ARCH11 as written. Take the target split from option 7 as the structural answer to the Swift-internal findings. Do not start options 1, 5 or 6. Run option 2 only as an out-of-tree format experiment, and only if the owner wants Rust for its own sake.

Order.

1. The audit's own fix list comes first and no option here touches it: G001, cross-meeting privacy (F014, F005, F046, F018), destructive operations (G003, F017, F016, F001, F020), F012, F004. Fix and pin the six Python highs before any port, so a port carries the fix rather than the defect.

2. In the same weeks, the neutral seam work: deletions (about 2.9k lines of Python plus FEAT18's cut), DIST6 with a version handshake, LOCAL21, ARCH10's single writer and result envelope, the routing fix for F022 and F023, generated contract fixtures.

3. Two owner-run measurements: the mlx-swift-lm candidate against mlx_lm on docs/spikes/local12_bench.py, and Neural Engine dispatch from the real menu-bar agent on macOS 27.2 together with qualifying a FluidAudio 0.18.x bump (ASR6).

4. Write ARCH11's boundary ADR with those numbers in it. If the candidate clears the four pre-registered bars, adopt staged all-Swift with a worker executable and make Apple Intelligence a provider of the Swift summarizer. If it does not, take ARCH11 as written (Python owns summarize and publish, the Swift copies are retired), keep DIST1's embedded runtime as the end state, and re-run the bench on each mlx-swift-lm minor release.

5. Target split and a real-recording test (option 7, stages 1 and 2).

Do the earlier NO-GO spikes still hold? The verdict holds until measured; its premise does not. DIST2 and LOCAL12 said no because an MLX-Swift summarizer would have to be hand-built: generation loop, tokenizers, Hugging Face loading, constrained decoding. The market file reports mlx-swift-lm 3.32.3 (30 September 2026) adding guided generation, multi-round tool calling, Qwen and Gemma 4 support and a Foundation Models conformer (https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx-swift-lm/releases), so the candidate can now be assembled in days and LOCAL12's own bench becomes runnable. Three things from the repo sharpen that. First, the Python baseline is not schema-constrained: summarize_local.py sends a response_format json_schema hint, but the mlx_lm 0.31.3 in the project venv has no handler for it (no reference in server.py or in any module except one chat template), and the module's own docstring says the constrained layer is still to come. JSON discipline today is prompt, three-layer extraction and a repair replay, so LOCAL12's fourth bar is lower than DIST2 assumed, and guided generation could remove repair loops rather than merely match them. Second, the price of waiting has risen: the pipeline was 13,942 lines when LOCAL12 was written and is 22,838 now. Third, the local path being protected is itself below the project's ship gate (action capture 0.42 to 0.56 against 0.80), so the bar is parity with mlx_lm, not excellence.

The Foundation Models LanguageModel protocol (https://developer.apple.com/documentation/updates/foundationmodels.md, https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2026/343/) is a good provider seam for a Swift summarizer but does not by itself reopen anything. It is OS 27 only against a macOS 14 package floor; vendor adapters carry their own network transport, so the single egress chokepoint has to be rebuilt and fenced; Private Cloud Compute must be excluded from regulated runs; and Apple's on-device model at 8,192 tokens on this Mac still cannot hold a one-hour transcript and failed Ukrainian in 3 of 5 runs on the assessment Mac, so DIST2's second trigger (Apple as the sole on-device summarizer) is not met. Neural Engine access from Rust does not bear on the Swift question; it closes option 1 for inference (the market file's source reports the ONNX Runtime CoreML provider crashing on Parakeet and mlx-rs as immature: https://moklabs-research.pages.dev/reports/technology/2026-03-20-parakeet-rs-tauri-integration-patterns, https://github.com/oxiglade/mlx-rs).

What I could not verify. I built and ran nothing and did no web research. Everything about mlx-swift-lm, the LanguageModel protocol, mlx-rs and ONNX Runtime comes from the market file's summaries; I did not read their source or release notes. Unknown until the spike: guided-generation quality and speed on Qwen2.5-7B-4bit with the MeetingSummary schema, tokenizer and chat-template parity, memory, whether mlx-swift builds under plain SwiftPM, whether a Swift equivalent of multilingual-e5-small exists for Ask, and the state of a Swift MCP SDK. Whether the menu-bar agent gets the Neural Engine on macOS 27.2 is unmeasured by the audit and by me. The seam sizes (about 7k lines of glue, 26 file kinds, 18 mirrored rules) are the audit finder's numbers; my own counts are wc and grep on the tree at 4949c9c0 (22,838 lines of Python source, 78,341 of Swift, 16 distinct mp verbs at Swift spawn sites). I classified six of the fourteen seam findings from titles and slice notes rather than full text. All effort figures are estimates, and the go-live figures are that assessment's, not re-derived.

## What to stop doing

The three judges' lists, kept separate because their overlap is the signal.

**market-skeptic.**

- Adding parity features to the base loop (detect, capture, transcribe, summarize, export, more read-only MCP tools): three market segments call it commodity and Minutes already lists 34 tools.
- Filing audits and tasks faster than fixes are verified: 18 claimed fixes do not hold, so adopt I46's witness rule and pause new audits until open critical and high findings are zero.
- Carrying undecided secondary surfaces: delete the Facts rail, prep card, workflow-keyed series continuity, App Intents and the LAN sink by I42's dated rule, with the People rail exempt.
- Destructive behaviour in the strictest mode and the default sink: the regulated real-time gate and held-tail cut (I41), and Notion wipe-and-append plus full transcript upload (I43).
- Claiming sensing breadth nobody has measured: browser tabs and never-recorded apps (I47).
- Reopening the language question without a number: no Rust in-tree (I07, I14, I20) and no Swift port (I17) until LOCAL12's bench has run once.
- Chasing where funded vendors are expanding: in-person, phone and watch capture (I19, I24, I25), pre-meeting briefs (I12), Spotlight and Siri parity (I13), live assistance.
- Designing probes that need owner hand-labelling as a precondition: prefer label-free instruments (I35) and keep no more than five owner-run measurements open at once.
- Writing invariants as prose or comments with no fence behind them (F041, G032, F129, F151).
- Maintaining two summarizers and dead local tooling: delete the LoRA trainer now, and decide the Apple Intelligence copy and the staged file after the bench and I06 (I44's deletions, not its pre-written boundary).

**codebase-engineer.**

- Running audits and filing tasks faster than fixes can be witnessed (about 134 tasks since 2026-10-21 and 18 fixes that do not hold); close a fix only with a test of intent that failed first or an event-log replay.
- Keeping the real-time regulated gate and the destructive stop-time cut; the strictest mode should not be the one that loses audio (G001, F004).
- Carrying undecided secondary surfaces: series continuity and the prep card (F014 lives there), the Facts rail, App Intents, the LAN sink.
- Wiping Notion pages on republish and uploading the full transcript by default (F017, F088, G032).
- Maintaining detector paths with zero recorded meetings (browser tabs, never-used native apps) while end-of-call fixes for the one real app are not holding.
- Keeping unwired or dev-only Python in the product tree: the staged summarizer without a dated verdict, and the LoRA trainer that trains on a request shape the server never sends (F083).
- Designing probes that need the owner to hand-label or hand-run a bench; prefer label-free, counts-only scripts and cap owner sittings at three a quarter.
- Adding per-meeting file kinds, cross-meeting readers or new capture surfaces before ARCH10 gives the file set one writer and the canary fences exist.
- Debating a Rust core, cross-platform or a mobile companion; the engine is Apple frameworks and the owner has one Mac.
- Letting docs and comments assert invariants no fence checks (README on mute redaction, SETUP on who sees the transcript, coverage.sh printing 90.0% for a daemon at 51.0%).
- Treating a probe that returns GO on the mechanism as done before the user-visible outcome has been seen (the App Intents lesson).

**owner-advocate.**

- Running audits and filing tasks faster than one person can verify them: eight audit documents and about 134 tasks in three weeks, while 18 fixes recorded as done do not hold.
- Closing work as partial or owner-verify without a witness (a test of intent that failed first, or an event-log replay).
- Carrying secondary surfaces with no recorded use (Facts rail, prep card, workflow-keyed series continuity, App Intents, LAN sink), and deciding the rest by opinion instead of a local open count.
- Destroying audio in real time under regulated mode; the strictest mode should not be the lossiest.
- Wiping and rewriting Notion pages on republish, and uploading the full transcript to a cloud workspace by default.
- Maintaining a second summarizer in Swift, an unwired staged summarizer and a trainer that trains on a request shape the server never sends.
- Promising detection for apps and browser tabs the event log has never seen, on a fixture of 21 seeded guesses and no captures.
- Writing invariants as prose (README, CONVENTIONS, long incident comments) that no fence checks.
- Building benches that wait on the owner to run them (LOCAL13, LOCAL14, CAL3 leg 1, DET6, T8 item 4): each becomes a script that prints a verdict in minutes or gets a deletion date.
- Holding or bumping the FluidAudio pin on faith; accept or reject on a scorecard diff taken on the owner's own audio.
- Debating rewrites without a number: Rust is closed on the evidence, and the Swift question is settled by one bench, not by argument.
- Adding per-meeting file kinds (about 26 today) before one typed owner for a meeting's file set exists.
- Chasing parity with funded vendors: briefs, mobile and watch capture, in-person notes, live transcripts.

## The market in late 2026

Six segments. Claims carry their sources; items the researchers could not verify are listed as such at the end of each segment.

### Cloud AI meeting notetakers, bot-based and botless

By late 2026 cloud meeting notetaking is a mature, overcrowded category in which capture, transcription and summaries are commodities. Money has concentrated at the top: Granola raised $125M at $1.5B (March), Wispr raised $280M at $2B and shipped a Granola-style notetaker (August), Otter took $320M of growth financing from General Catalyst's Customer Value Fund (October), and Fireflies sits at a reported $1B valuation. Consolidation has started: Superhuman (formerly Grammarly) bought Fathom in September, and CRM, HR and sales-engagement vendors bought smaller players. Capture has shifted from visible bots to device audio: nearly every bot-first vendor now offers a botless desktop mode, Supernormal moved capture to its desktop app, and Microsoft Teams now labels and can auto-block external bots. Platforms bundle the basics (Zoom My Notes, Notion, Calendly, Copilot) and Google released an on-device Mac notetaker. Vendors therefore compete on what happens around the meeting: being the context layer for AI agents (MCP servers are near universal), pre-meeting briefs, post-meeting recipes, CRM autofill, deal intelligence, coaching scorecards and voice agents that run calls. Pricing has converged on a free tier plus roughly $8 to $20 per user per month, with $25 to $40 for business and enterprise tiers. The unresolved problems are consent and trust: wiretap class actions now cover bot-based Otter and Fireflies and botless Granola, tl;dv exposed metadata for 181,874 meetings, and surveyed users rank accuracy on names, numbers and speaker identification first, with 59% planning to switch tools within a year.

**What it means for this product.**

- Crowded, do not expect distinction here: detect a call, capture system audio plus mic without a bot, transcribe and diarize on device, summarize, export to Notion or Obsidian, expose an MCP server. Talat ships almost exactly this stack (FluidAudio, Parakeet, local or BYO LLM, Obsidian export, webhooks, MCP) for a one-time fee, Google gives away an on-device Mac notetaker, and open-source clones appear weekly. The base loop is a commodity on both the cloud and the local side (https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/24/talats-ai-meeting-notes-stay-on-your-machine-not-in-the-cloud/, https://techcrunch.com/2026/10/08/google-releases-a-new-local-first-granola-competitor/, https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/items/50454625).
- What the cloud leaders ship that a solo local tool cannot easily match, so do not chase it: shared team workspaces and cross-colleague search, CRM field sync and deal intelligence, phone, Android and watch capture, 30+ languages, voice agents that conduct calls, enterprise admin (SSO, SCIM, audit), directory-listed connectors, and bot capture of video and screen share. All of these depend on multi-user data, mobile clients or a sales motion (https://granola.ai/updates, https://investor.wedbush.com/wedbush/article/gnwcq-2026-7-21-fireflies-launches-ai-sales-suite-bringing-enterprise-sales-intelligence-to-its-20-million-users-at-a-fraction-of-what-legacy-platforms-charge, https://www.avoma.com/pricing).
- The reverse asset is retained local audio. Granola deletes audio after transcription and is considering expiring verbatim transcripts; a local tool that keeps the recording can re-transcribe and re-diarize its whole archive whenever models improve, play back the exact moment behind any claim, and correct names and numbers after the fact. That is the one thing the best-funded botless vendor has chosen not to offer, and it maps directly onto users' top-ranked complaint (names, numbers, speaker identity) (https://www.granola.ai/pricing, https://platformer.news/granola-chris-pedregal-interview, https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map).
- Thin area, agent interface depth rather than agent interface existence. Everyone has an MCP server, but most are read-only, plan-gated or limited to recent notes, and Granola is only now adding a daily 'Agent brief'. A local server with no gating can expose richer primitives: evidence-linked answers with audio timestamps, commitments and decisions tracked across meetings, per-person and per-project timelines, and write-back tools. With cloud vendors positioning as the context layer, the interesting question is what a personal context layer can do when it owns raw audio and has no retention policy to honour (https://www.granola.ai/pricing, https://granola.ai/updates, https://meetingnotes.com/blog/ai-meeting-notes-with-mcp-and-ai-agent-integration).
- Thin area, consent as a designed feature. The litigation now targets invisibility itself, vendors push the duty onto the user, and no vendor has a good answer because the meeting platforms expose no consent API. A personal tool can be deliberately visible on its owner's terms: automatic disclosure message, per-contact or per-jurisdiction rules, a record of who was told, and a hard pause for sensitive calls. Running locally removes the vendor from the picture but not the user's own notice obligations, so this is worth building rather than assuming away (https://btlaw.com/en/insights/alerts/2026/what-the-granola-class-action-means-for-companies-building-and-deploying-conversation-capture-tools, https://platformer.news/granola-chris-pedregal-interview).
- Speaker identity is both the second-ranked user priority and the subject of surviving BIPA voiceprint claims against a cloud vendor. A single-user tool that keeps speaker profiles on device, learns recurring voices across meetings, and ties them to calendar attendees can go further on cross-meeting identity than vendors who now have legal reasons to be cautious about holding voiceprints. Commenters on local tools report this is still unreliable, so quality here would be noticed (https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map, https://uctoday.com/productivity-automation/otter-ai-fails-to-dismiss-core-privacy-claims-in-u-s-court/, https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/items/50454625).
- The market's energy is in preparation and follow-through (briefs, recipes, workflows), yet those features in cloud tools draw only on the vendor's own silo plus a few connectors. A one-user tool can be opinionated instead of general: briefs built from the owner's own archive and Obsidian vault, follow-ups written in the owner's voice, and checks of what was promised last time against what happened. Depth for one person beats breadth for a team (https://granola.ai/updates, https://www.zoom.com/en/products/ai-assistant/features/ai-note-taking).
- Radical directions the field leaves open: treat the recorder as a durable personal archive with an open on-disk format that outlives any vendor (59% of users plan to switch tools within a year and acquisitions like Fathom's leave free plans in doubt); make every summary sentence auditable against audio; and optimise for trust properties cloud vendors cannot claim after the tl;dv exposure, namely no account, no multi-tenant store, no training use. Price and botless capture are not differentiators, since free tiers and device capture are everywhere (https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map, https://blog.superhuman.com/superhuman-acquires-fathom/, https://tldv.io/blog/our-thoughts-on-the-darkreading-com-article/).

**Trends.**

- Consolidation into suites has begun. Superhuman (formerly Grammarly) bought Fathom (Sep 2026); Salesforce bought Momentum (Mar 2026); Workday bought Sana (late 2025, about $1.1B); Lemlist bought Claap (2025); Aircall bought Piper (Jun 2026); Metaview bought Reval (Aug 2026); Rogo bought Arvo. The buyers want meeting data as agent memory inside a larger product, not a standalone notes app. <https://blog.superhuman.com/superhuman-acquires-fathom/> <https://www.gladia.io/blog/ai-notetaker-market-funding-and-acquisitions> <https://dealroom.co/news/148503-rogo-buys-ai-meeting-assistant-arvo-to-feed-deal-teams-data/>
- Capital is concentrating in a few names while the long tail bootstraps: Granola $125M at $1.5B (Mar 2026), Wispr $280M at $2B (Aug 2026), Otter $320M in customer-acquisition financing (Oct 2026), Fireflies at $1B via a 2025 tender. Circleback (8 people, profitable), tl;dv and Jamie run on little or no venture money. <https://thenextweb.com/news/granola-series-c-meeting-ai-enterprise-context> <https://thenextweb.com/news/wispr-series-b-280m-2bn-valuation-menlo-canto> <https://thenextweb.com/news/otter-ai-320m-general-catalyst> <https://www.gladia.io/blog/ai-notetaker-market-funding-and-acquisitions>
- Botless device-audio capture went from Granola's differentiator to table stakes. Fathom (beta, Mac), tl;dv, Circleback, Fellow, Avoma, Krisp, Supernormal, Notion, Zoom My Notes and Wispr all offer it; Recall.ai sells a Desktop Recording SDK so any product can add it. <https://fathom.ai/pricing> <https://circleback.ai/pricing> <https://www.avoma.com/pricing> <https://www.supernormal.com/product-updates>
- Platforms are squeezing visible bots. Microsoft Teams labels external bots 'Unverified', holds them in the lobby by default, and from August 2026 lets admins block detected bots outright; commentary notes this pushes tenants toward Copilot. Bot-first vendors must register bots with Microsoft or fall back to desktop capture. <https://www.uctoday.com/unified-communications/microsoft-teams-to-block-external-bots-automatically-as-ai-notetaker-crackdown-hardens/> <https://office365itpros.com/2026/08/24/teams-meeting-notetaking-bots/> <https://blog-en.topedia.com/2026/05/meeting-bot-detection-in-microsoft-teams/>
- The pitch has moved from 'notes' to 'context layer for agents'. MCP servers are now near universal (Granola, Otter, Fireflies, Circleback, Fellow, Avoma, Read AI, Krisp, Jamie, Tactiq, Supernormal, Wispr), often plan-gated: Granola limits free MCP to 30 days of notes, Jamie reserves it for Pro, Tactiq for Business, while Otter and Circleback include it free. Granola's CEO calls MCP usage its fastest-growing metric. <https://www.granola.ai/pricing> <https://otter.ai/pricing> <https://circleback.ai/pricing> <https://www.meetjamie.ai/pricing>
- Product effort has moved to before and after the meeting: Granola Briefs, Recipes and a daily Agent brief; Fireflies Meeting Prep and CRM Autofill; Zoom Workflows for recap emails and tasks; Supernormal generating slides and spreadsheets from meetings. <https://granola.ai/updates> <https://investor.wedbush.com/wedbush/article/gnwcq-2026-7-21-fireflies-launches-ai-sales-suite-bringing-enterprise-sales-intelligence-to-its-20-million-users-at-a-fraction-of-what-legacy-platforms-charge> <https://www.zoom.com/en/products/ai-assistant/features/ai-note-taking> <https://www.supernormal.com/product-updates>
- Leaders go vertical and upmarket for margin: sales intelligence, scorecards against MEDDIC/BANT/SPICED, deal risk, recruiting and support voice agents. Fireflies undercuts legacy revenue-intelligence contracts; Avoma sells it as add-ons; Fathom gates CRM field sync and coaching to Business. <https://investor.wedbush.com/wedbush/article/gnwcq-2026-7-21-fireflies-launches-ai-sales-suite-bringing-enterprise-sales-intelligence-to-its-20-million-users-at-a-fraction-of-what-legacy-platforms-charge> <https://thenextweb.com/news/fireflies-voice-agents-job-screening-eu-ai-act-annex-iii-high-risk> <https://www.avoma.com/pricing> <https://fathom.ai/pricing>
- Free tiers are the customer-acquisition channel and prices have converged. Circleback and Granola both added free tiers in 2026; Fathom and Otter keep theirs; paid individual plans cluster at $8 to $20 per month and business or enterprise at $25 to $40. Krisp is the exception, replacing its free plan with a 7-day trial. <https://techcrunch.com/2026/08/31/meeting-notetaker-circleback-adds-a-free-tier-to-attract-more-customers/> <https://www.granola.ai/pricing> <https://fathom.ai/pricing> <https://otter.ai/pricing>
- Adjacent products and platforms now bundle a notetaker: Zoom My Notes works across Teams and Meet and in person without a bot (free tier of 3 uses per month, standalone plan from $8.33); Notion includes botless AI Meeting Notes in its $20 Business plan; Calendly launched Notetaker with its Callie assistant on 19 Aug 2026; Teams Copilot can produce a recap without saving a transcript. <https://www.zoom.com/en/products/ai-assistant/features/ai-note-taking> <https://www.notion.com/pricing> <https://techcrunch.com/2026/08/19/calendly-throws-its-hat-into-meeting-note-taker-circus/> <https://www.itelio.com/en/microsoft-message-center/MC1275312>
- Local-first is no longer a fringe position. Google released AI Edge Foresight, an experimental offline Mac notetaker built on Gemma models (Oct 2026); Talat sells a one-time-purchase Mac app on FluidAudio and Parakeet with Obsidian export, webhooks and an MCP server; an open-source Granola alternative hit 271 points on Hacker News on 11 Nov 2026, where commenters already call the local category saturated. <https://techcrunch.com/2026/10/08/google-releases-a-new-local-first-granola-competitor/> <https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/24/talats-ai-meeting-notes-stay-on-your-machine-not-in-the-cloud/> <https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/items/50454625> <https://github.com/FrigadeHQ/yogurt>

**Complaints and gaps.**

- Uninvited bots remain the most visible irritation: admins on Zoom's forum describe kicking several notetaker bots out of every meeting, and a university IT desk warns of bots joining discreetly and recording sensitive content. <https://community.zoom.com/t5/Zoom-Meetings/How-do-i-disable-AI-Notetakers-otter-ai-read-ai-fireflies-ai-etc/m-p/233848> <https://kb.wisc.edu/helpdesk/149383>
- Going botless does not solve consent, it hides it. Chamberlain v. Granola (N.D. Cal., filed 30 Jul 2026) is brought by a non-user recorded by someone else's app and treats the absence of notice as the violation; Granola's notice options (chat message, video watermark) are off by default and the vendor puts consent duty on the user. Granola's CEO says Zoom and Meet offer no API to ask participants for consent. <https://btlaw.com/en/insights/alerts/2026/what-the-granola-class-action-means-for-companies-building-and-deploying-conversation-capture-tools> <https://platformer.news/granola-chris-pedregal-interview> <https://www.layer3labs.io/guides/ai-note-taker-lawsuits>
- Vendor use of meeting data is the legal pressure point. The Otter court found it plausibly alleged that Otter used conversations for its own commercial purposes (model improvement) and let wiretap and BIPA voiceprint claims proceed; Granola uses anonymized data for model improvement on free and business plans unless each user opts out. <https://uctoday.com/productivity-automation/otter-ai-fails-to-dismiss-core-privacy-claims-in-u-s-court/> <https://btlaw.com/en/insights/alerts/2026/what-the-granola-class-action-means-for-companies-building-and-deploying-conversation-capture-tools> <https://www.granola.ai/pricing>
- Multi-tenant cloud stores of meetings are an attack surface. tl;dv's flaw let any signed-up user enumerate metadata for 181,874 meetings and obtain live-call IDs while the product was marketed as SOC 2 compliant; reviewers also flag Granola's link-sharing defaults and US-only hosting. <https://runtimewire.com/article/tldv-firestore-flaw-exposed-meeting-records-live-call-ids> <https://tldv.io/blog/our-thoughts-on-the-darkreading-com-article/> <https://www.pulse.bot/cybersecurity/news/psa-anyone-with-a-link-can-view-your-granola-notes-by-default-328aa849-2ed0-4696-95ae-db4dba257acd> <https://blog.buildbetter.ai/do-they-own-your-data-granola-ai-privacy-policy-reviewed/>
- Accuracy still fails where it matters. In a 2,100-user survey (OnePoll for Gladia, Mar 2026) the top-ranked priorities were accurate names and numbers, accurate speaker identification, accurate summaries and multilingual transcription, ahead of botless capture (fifth) and integrations; 48% are frequently in multilingual meetings. <https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map>
- Loyalty is weak: 59% of surveyed users plan to switch assistants within 12 months, only about one in three chose their tool themselves, and no independent tool except one exceeds 20% awareness against Teams, Meet and Zoom's native features. <https://gladia.io/meeting-assistant-market-map>
- Botless modes in bot-first products are second class: tl;dv's desktop mode captures audio only with no video, screen share or chat; Fathom's bot-free option is a Mac-only beta; Fellow's requires the desktop app; Tactiq captures no audio at all. <https://www.laxis.com/blog/tldv-review/> <https://fathom.ai/pricing> <https://fellow.ai/blog/bot-free-ai-note-takers/> <https://www.laxis.com/blog/tactiq-review/>
- Pricing is full of meters and traps: tl;dv's free limits are lifetime, not monthly; Fireflies meters AI features with credits that pause when exhausted; Jamie caps notes per month and meeting length; Avoma's useful sales features roughly quadruple the base seat price; free tiers at Granola and Circleback keep only 30 days of history. <https://www.laxis.com/blog/tldv-review/> <https://sonix.ai/resources/fireflies-ai-pricing/> <https://www.meetjamie.ai/pricing> <https://resources.rework.com/tools/ai-tools/best-avoma-alternatives>
- Cloud tools that delete audio after transcription (Granola states this on every tier) leave users with a transcript they cannot re-check against the recording or re-process with better models; Granola is also studying automatic deletion of verbatim transcripts in favour of compressed memory. <https://www.granola.ai/pricing> <https://platformer.news/granola-chris-pedregal-interview>
- Hacker News users of Granola-class tools voice subscription fatigue and privacy unease, and ask local alternatives for model choice, non-English support, optional cloud summarization, lower memory use than Electron, authenticated local MCP, reliable diarization and speaker names, and consent prompts when new participants join. Platformer's interviewer adds the surveillance and 'notes for other AIs to summarize' critique. <https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/items/50454625> <https://platformer.news/granola-chris-pedregal-interview>

**Not verified.**

- Search budget note: the shared web-search limit was reached after about 36 searches, so a few planned verifications were not completed. Everything below is unverified or only partly verified.
- Otter bot-free capture: I could not confirm whether Otter's desktop app records without a bot. Otter's pricing page describes the assistant joining meetings; one third-party comparison said Otter and Fireflies now offer bot-free desktop modes, but I did not verify this on a primary page.
- Fireflies list prices are from third-party trackers (Pro about $10 annual / $18 monthly, Business $19 / $29, Enterprise $39); the vendor pricing page did not show prices to my fetch. Status and details of the Fireflies class action (Parrinello v. Fireflies.AI Corp., No. 3:26-cv-02479, N.D. Cal.) come from search snippets and a secondary guide, not the docket.
- Supernormal paid pricing: a search snippet reported $18 per member per month (Oct 2026) but the vendor page rendered paid tiers as $0, so the figure is unconfirmed.
- Fathom deal status: Superhuman's own post says it 'has acquired' Fathom, while one outlet framed it as an agreement with no stated closing date. The 400,000 monthly-active-users figure and the $94M 2024 valuation are from secondary reports of TechCrunch and PitchBook that I did not read directly.
- tl;dv price change: a tracker reports the top tier rising from $59 to $98 per user per month in April 2026, which conflicts with the vendor page (Business EUR 39 monthly). Treat as unresolved. The Dark Reading article on the tl;dv flaw returned 403; numbers are from other coverage and tl;dv's own response.
- A July 2026 survey of 500 US workers (about a third had an AI notetaker in their meetings, 65% of those not consistently asked first) appeared in a search summary without a clear originating URL; not verified.
- A claim that only 41% of users use their assistant to trigger a workflow was attributed to a Gladia report in a search summary; the report page I fetched did not show that figure.
- ChatGPT record mode: search snippets from OpenAI's help center say it remains macOS-only for paid plans with a 4-hour cap and a 'ChatGPT Meetings' desktop app is mentioned; the help page itself returned 403 so I could not confirm. Google Gemini 'take notes for me' in Meet was not researched.
- A 2025 Granola incident (an exposed transcription-provider API key affecting 333 iOS beta testers) appeared in a search summary of third-party safety reviews; I did not verify it against a primary source.
- Read AI: no 2026 funding or acquisition was found, but absence of results is not proof. The 'MCP in open beta' claim and several MCP comparisons come from a blog that heavily promotes Fellow; that same blog wrongly says Granola has no official MCP, which Granola's own changelog contradicts (official MCP launched 4 Feb 2026).
- Recall.ai: a $38M Series B at $250M (Sep 2025) and about $31M ARR (Jan 2026) are from Sacra and press; a reported March 2026 price cut to $0.50 per recording hour is from a single unverified job-board page.
- From memory, not verified in this session: Limitless (formerly Rewind) was acquired by Meta in late 2025 and wound down its pendant sales. No shutdowns were found among the thirteen named leads; Supernormal's changelog has been silent since 12 Jun 2026, which may or may not mean anything.
- Jamie's funding status (described as bootstrapped) and Fellow 5.0's release date were not confirmed from primary sources. Wispr Notetaker's processing location (cloud or device) and the date of its Windows release are not stated in the sources I read; the architecture label 'cloud' for it is an assumption.

### Local-first, privacy-first and open-source meeting recorders, especially on macOS

As of November 2026 the "local Granola alternative" on Apple Silicon is a commodity category. FluidAudio (Apache-2.0) plus Parakeet gives any Swift developer on-device ASR and diarization; its showcase lists 51 apps, about 16 of them meeting recorders, and GitHub's parakeet topic lists 603 repositories. At least a dozen products now ship the same shape as the owner's tool: botless system-plus-mic capture, on-device transcription, speaker labels, a small bundled LLM or bring-your-own key, Markdown or Obsidian export and an MCP server (Talat, Muesli, Daisy, Ghosty Notes, Summit, meeting-transcriber, OpenOats, Anarlog, Meetily). Dictation apps joined in 2026 (MacWhisper 15.2, Superwhisper Meetings, Wispr Flow Notetaker) and platforms bundle botless capture (ChatGPT Record, Notion, Apple Notes). The field is unstable: Hyprnote became Char and then Anarlog while its team moved on to another product; Rewind was switched off after Meta bought Limitless; screenpipe went source-available with subscriptions; Talat moved from a 49 dollar one-time price to subscription and lifetime tiers; Granola (1.5 billion dollar valuation) locked its local cache and now faces a class action over undisclosed botless recording. Open-core projects gate diarization, auto-detection and MCP behind paid tiers. Thin areas: trustworthy local summaries (vendors admit small models hallucinate and miss action items), cross-meeting memory and commitments, permissioned agent write-back, consent tooling, multilingual and code-switched speech, and the long-term durability of a user-owned archive.

**What it means for this product.**

- Crowded (inference from the player map): the owner's baseline feature set, meaning call detection, botless system-plus-mic capture, Parakeet on the Neural Engine via FluidAudio, diarization, local LLM or BYO key, Obsidian/Markdown export, searchable library and a read-only MCP server, is now shipped by Talat, Muesli, Daisy, Ghosty Notes, Summit, meeting-transcriber and others, free or for under 200 dollars. More work on parity features will not make the tool distinctive; treat that layer as solved plumbing.
- Runtime language is not where differentiation lives (inference). Meetily is a Rust/Tauri app and is distinctive for cross-platform reach, not quality, while the strongest Apple Silicon ASR and diarization path (FluidAudio, CoreML, Neural Engine) is Swift-native. A Rust rewrite would buy portability to Windows and Linux, which matters only if the owner wants those platforms, and would cost the Neural Engine integration that the best Mac tools rely on.
- Thin: trustworthy local summaries. Vendors admit small models hallucinate and miss action items and nobody publishes measured accuracy. A personal tool with a private archive can do what a vendor cannot easily do: evidence-linked notes where every decision and action item cites transcript timestamps, an automatic verification pass that flags unsupported claims, and a regression eval over the owner's own past meetings to pick models and prompts per meeting type.
- Thin: longitudinal memory rather than per-meeting notes. Local tools mostly stop at a summary plus search; cloud players are moving to pre-meeting briefs and cross-meeting Q and A (Wispr Flow, Granola, Ghosty's hosted Pro model). Fully local tracking of commitments, decisions and open questions per person and project, with a brief generated before the next call with the same people, is still rare on device.
- Thin: the agent surface beyond read-only MCP. MCP read access is table stakes; the interesting designs are permissioned and logged write-back (Ghosty Notes), CLI plus skill files and post-meeting hooks (Muesli), and per-agent scoping (cloud vendors). A single-user tool can go further by treating the meeting archive as a local work-memory service with scoped tokens, audit log and redaction rules for sensitive meetings.
- Durability is a structural advantage worth making explicit. 2026 saw a shutdown (Rewind), two renames and a team refocus (Anarlog), a relicensing (screenpipe), a pricing model change (Talat) and a local cache lock (Granola). A tool that is not for sale can guarantee plain files, documented formats, no licence server and tested backup and restore, which is the one promise commercial players keep breaking.
- Consent and retention tooling is newly relevant and thin. The Granola suit attacks invisible capture with notice off by default, and open-source tools mostly offer a one-time acknowledgement at best. Distinctive options for a personal tool: one-step disclosure (chat message or invite text), per-contact or per-jurisdiction rules, automatic audio deletion after transcription as Granola and Jamie do, and a mode that refuses to record flagged meetings.
- Two capability bets where local tools are behind: (1) multilingual and code-switched meetings, where Parakeet v3 is limited to 25 European languages and fails on switching, so per-meeting engine routing across Parakeet, Whisper, Qwen3-ASR or Apple SpeechAnalyzer could matter if the owner's calls mix languages; (2) live assistance during the call (retrieval from the owner's own library, catch-up recap), which among local tools only OpenOats really attempts.
